  - The whole-device tests (`system_clock`, `embed`, `heartbeat_walk`, `peer`, dlink `link` and `rx_pull`, dstack-asio `to_asio_pull`), `mdns_answer_only`, the mDNS meter's socket test and the heartbeat listener's tests (`latency`, which sent made-up heartbeats to 224.0.0.233:8708) run on 127.0.0.1. Measured after (all of them, 150 s): 0 datagrams on the Dante leg, 2 on the LAN leg (not ours: no device of ours was on it).
- **Production and consumers: no change.** Without the variable the host refuses loopback as before (the binary, dasio, dlink apps). A consumer's own tests can opt in the same way (its own `.cargo/config.toml` `[env]`). New public API (additive): `host::test_run` (`TestRun` with `from_env`, `from_vars`, `loopback`, `admit`; `loopback_net`, `LOOPBACK_ENV`, `LIVE_ENV`, `SITE_REFUSED`). **No latency change.**
- **A live test from this workspace** (`cargo run --bin dstack-dev -- --ip <a real address>`) now needs `DSTACK_LIVE_TEST=1`; a built binary started directly has no variable and runs as before.

## 0.6.163

- **Tests (#349): the channel-change notification's walker rule is the hardware's now, not inferno's.** Captured on the info group (224.0.0.231:8702) after dstack's own ARC client renamed an RX channel and back (restored at once, logged on the ticket): an UltimoX2 (channel 1 of 2) and a Yamaha card (Audinate DCM, channels 7 and 8 of 128). The hardware sends a `u16` mask length n, then n mask bytes, nothing after (no padding to 4: 35 and 36 bytes), bit `ch % 8` of byte `ch / 8` for RX channel `ch` from 0, the shortest mask that holds the highest channel named (1 byte for channel 7 of 128), one notification per rename (the rename back too, none empty). The opcode's second byte is the device's own (0x38 on the UltimoX2, 0x24 on the Yamaha card; ours 0x2a). The strict walker (`tests/conformance/walk.rs`) now also refuses a mask whose last byte names no channel and matches the opcode by its bytes 2-3; the captures are `channel_changes` in `tests/fixtures/heartbeat_hardware.json` (device ids zeros), and our notification for the channels the hardware named equals the hardware's body and length byte for byte. Our writer already sent these bytes: no fix. Test code and docs only; **no latency change**, no API change.
- **Tests (#339, its fifth case): the Windows timer tests name the runner's timer in their failure.** v0.6.157's tag CI (run 43961897282, attempt 2) failed `clock::sys`'s `a_timer_wait_of_1_ms_ends_within_1_ms_at_the_median` and `a_wait_on_the_app_clock_never_ends_before_its_target_on_it` with every wait about 1.01 ms late at the median: a VM whose timer fires only on its 1 ms resolution. Their failure now carries the runner's timer state (`NtQueryTimerResolution`'s current, finest and coarsest resolution, and whether the thread got its high-resolution waitable timer) and a verdict: `RUNNER: coarse timer (res X ms)` when the median oversleep is within a quarter of the resolution, `OURS` when the resolution is known and does not explain it (or the wait was spun), `UNKNOWN` when it could not be read or the thread had no high-resolution timer. Such a run still fails. The decision is the private `clock::timer_state` (called only by tests, unit-tested on Linux); `windows-sys`'s `Wdk_System_SystemInformation` feature is a Windows dev-dependency only (test builds; no new crate).
- **CI (#339): the ci-peer gate takes a pump's oversleep as runner evidence beside its witness.** `tools/linux/tests/ci_peer_gate.py` now reads each peer's log too: a late re-bootstrap the witness did not see is voided when its own pump watch line (one re-bootstrap, `cause=late`, the second's latest wake its own) shows its sleep returned past the margin W and within 0.5 ms of the wake's whole lateness (`oversleep_max_us`, #354, v0.6.158): the thread was not run when its timer fired (sound on the peers' test system clock, which never steps). A thread's CPU against its wall time is not used (a Hyper-V runner counts host steal as the thread's CPU). Main run 43961883405 (v0.6.157, a lone 6125 us re-bootstrap) had no such field and stands as before; the same line with a 6090 us oversleep would be void (unit tests on both).
- **No latency change**; no API change. Consumers: nothing to port.

## 0.6.162

- **Internal (#360): `media/tx.rs` split, no change.** A pure move: `Schedule` and its time helpers into `media/tx/schedule.rs`, the TX thread (`Transmitter`, `spawn` and its loops) into `media/tx/thread.rs`, the control side's `TxTable`/`TxFlow` into `media/tx/table.rs`, the OS thread calls (priority, pinning) into `media/tx/priority.rs`. Every public name keeps its `dstack::media::tx::` path (re-exported); no behaviour, API or latency change.

## 0.6.161

- **Fix (#358): `dstack-dev peer` no longer counts its own pump's skip as a break of the tone it receives.** The peer checked every block of the received tone with no regard to its own block start, so when its engine pump re-bootstrapped (skipped the blocks it was too late for) the positions it never read made the tone jump, and the check logged one 2-sample `kind=misplaced` tone break and counted it in `tone_gaps` (measured at obs's shape, 440 Hz at 96 kHz, 128-frame blocks: a skip of 1, 5, 6 or 11 blocks, one break each; obs's soak run had 22 peer re-bootstraps and 21 such breaks, so an app's gate failed on the test peer's own stall). Now the peer takes its engine's own signal that its inputs jumped (`Process::discontinuity`, raised before the block after a re-bootstrap, a retime, an off-grid re-anchor or a start after free pacing): it re-locks each channel's check (`ToneCheck::relock`: the next block is judged from its third sample on against the last clean fit, so a break of the received flow right after the jump still counts; only a break exactly at it cannot be told apart; a gap open at the jump goes on) and counts the jump apart, from the second start on: status `peer.own_skips`, and one WARN a jump (target `dstack::peer::tone`, `dstack-dev peer: its own reads jumped; the tone check re-locks`, fields `frames`, the signed jump from the end of the last block with a media start, absent for a block with none, and `own_skips`). A block start that moves with no discontinuity (a re-anchor kept on the grid, the inputs unbroken) is no own jump. On the peer's test system clock every own jump is a re-bootstrap, and the status reads `own_skips` before the device's counts, so it never exceeds `device.engine.rebootstraps`. `docs/ci-peer.md` says how a gate reads it (a rise is the peer's stall, gated with `device.engine.rebootstraps`). dstack's own `ci-peer` gate is unchanged: it already gates on the peer's re-bootstraps with the runner-stall witness.
- **Docs (#354, obs#69): a block's thread CPU time near its wall time does not prove work (on a VM above all).** obs's soak on v0.6.159 (GitHub runner, Azure, kernel `7.0.0-1019-azure`) logged four `cause=none` seconds whose largest block had CPU = wall (1544-3212 us). That runner's kernel, like Ubuntu's 7.0 generic one, has neither `CONFIG_PARAVIRT_TIME_ACCOUNTING` nor `CONFIG_IRQ_TIME_ACCOUNTING`, and Hyper-V gives the guest no steal clock (Linux registers one for KVM, none for Hyper-V), so the time the host took the virtual CPU, the interrupts that ran on it and the softirqs run on their exit count as the thread's CPU time, and `/proc/stat` steal stays 0. On such a guest no counter separates host steal from work, so no steal figure was added (it would read 0 on the runners where the question arises; a KVM guest does count steal in `/proc/stat`, a possible follow-up); inside a block, which takes no lock and never sleeps, wall less CPU is already the run queue wait that schedstat's `run_delay` would give. The same block shape on dev1 under `chrt -f 10` (obs's processor in dlink `Rated` on dstack's pump, stalls of 8, 50, 200 and 1000 ms, each re-bootstrapping and resyncing the TX player) took at most 56 us; the block after a re-bootstrap 30 us at the median. The `split` module and `PumpView::max_process_cpu_us` docs say so.
- **Read from the evidence (#354): the same soak's 14 tone breaks with no pump late wake were not the stack's work.** obs's `tx_resyncs` equals its `rebootstraps` on every status line, every re-bootstrap is a logged late wake, and none of the 14 has one within -50..+250 ms, so none is a TX resync. The nine 2-sample `kind=misplaced` breaks match the dstack-dev peer's own pump re-bootstraps, whose skipped reads make the peer's tone check see one phase jump: measured, a skip of 1, 5, 6 or 11 128-frame blocks of obs's 440 Hz tone at 96 kHz each gives exactly one 2-sample `misplaced` break; the peer had 22 re-bootstraps and the run 21 such breaks, two clean runs 0 and 0. The ~9-10 ms `kind=silence` breaks each followed `the TX schedule starts again` on obs's TX thread by 10-11 ms (lag 820-932 samples over its 803-sample latency): the TX thread, not the pump, was not run, and a packet that late is dropped by design (one 2-sample break, 01:15:23.861, has the same timestamp as such a restart). A TX resync's own skip in the bridge player (#303's re-prime, by design) starts the audio again where the blocks the pump lost end, so it joins that silence break (a gap ends only after 16 good samples) rather than making one of its own.
- **Feature (#359, part of #354): the TX thread's `the TX schedule starts again` split by cause, measured.** The line said how late the TX thread read the clock (`lag`) past the receiver's latency (`max_lag`), not why; in obs's soak (run 43990478131) five ~9-10 ms silences each followed such a restart. The TX thread now records:
  - **its sleep's oversleep**: how late its sleep returned past the time it asked for (the media clock read right before and right after the sleep, one clock epoch; a sleep asked for a time already past records none): scheduled late;
  - **each pass's own wall time** (the plans, the keepalives and every packet due): pass long;
  - **each pass's thread CPU time** (Linux, `CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID`; unmeasured elsewhere): a pass far longer than its CPU time was preempted (CPU near wall does not prove work on a VM, #354).
  - The restart line (additive fields after `max_lag`): `oversleep_us` (the sleep before the pass that restarts), `last_pass_us` and `last_pass_cpu_us` (the pass before that sleep), `-` when unmeasured (a sleep asked for a time already past, a send with no sleep before it such as the bounded escape's last pass, the relay thread's first pass after an escape, off Linux for the CPU). The relay thread that covers a bounded escape (Windows) times its passes and sleeps the same way. Example: `the TX schedule starts again lag=827 max_lag=803 oversleep_us=8512 last_pass_us=41 last_pass_cpu_us=40` reads scheduled late.
  - Status (additive, top level after `tx_bytes`): `tx_thread` `{oversleep_max_us, pass_max_us, pass_max_cpu_us}`, peaks since the device started (`pass_max_cpu_us` absent where unmeasured).
  - API (additive, dstack): `media::tx::split` (`TxThreadStats`, `TxThreadView`, `Before`, `Us`), `TxShared::tx_thread`, `Transmitter::timed_step` and `Transmitter::slept` (the TX loop's wiring), `Schedule::due_after` (`due` keeps its signature).
  - Cost on the TX thread: two `Instant` reads and, on Linux, two thread CPU clock reads a pass (system calls, no vDSO path); two media clock reads and one epoch read around each sleep; relaxed `fetch_max`, no lock, no allocation (`tests/hot_path.rs` runs the timed passes under `assert_no_alloc`).
- **No latency change**; the only API change is #359's additions. Consumers: nothing to port; a status or log reader may show the new fields.
  - #358: nothing to port; an app CI that gates on the peer's `tone_gaps` may add `own_skips` beside its re-bootstrap check. API: new public `peer::tone::ToneCheck::relock` and field `peer::Shared::own_skips` (a source break only for code that builds `Shared` by struct literal). **No latency change.**

## 0.6.160

- **Fix (#340): the info server survives a long multicast send failure and logs it once a minute, not once a packet.** On a Windows PC after a reboot (dsg, 15.-16.11.2026) every heartbeat (224.0.0.233:8708) and info packet (224.0.0.231:8702) the info server sent failed with WSAEADDRNOTAVAIL (10049) for about 24 hours, while the device's address and unicast worked: Dante Controller and dcontroller lost the device's heartbeat and info, and the log got one WARN a packet (11 678 lines). A new socket (the app's restart) did not clear it, an adapter restart did; the cause inside Windows is unproven. Now (`info::sendwatch`):
  - the log: a failed send is one WARN at once when no such line went within the last minute (`info packets could not be sent (counted; one line a minute at most)`, with `failures` and the OS `error`), else it is counted into the next one; at most one line a minute even when failures and sends alternate. The first send that goes out after a recovery logs `info multicast sends work again` with the outage's failures (those not yet reported included).
  - the recovery, best effort (it may well not clear dsg's state, which a new socket did not): 3 failures in a row name the socket's multicast interface again, by the address, and on Windows, when that is refused, by the index of the address's interface now (`IP_MULTICAST_IF`'s index form, 0.0.0.<index>); 10 in a row reopen the socket on `<ip>:8700`. Each kind waits a period since its last: a minute at first, doubled at each reopen up to an hour while the failures go on (a day like dsg's: about 30 reopens, not 1440); a send that goes out starts the minute again. The manager frees its loopback share port with the info port during the reopen and binds both again; if another device on the address takes the info port in that moment, it becomes the manager and this one its member (the share protocol as before), and when the port cannot be bound again for any reason this device waits as a member until it can. Members keep their registration across a manager's reopen. The watch is the socket's: when only one group fails while the other's sends go out, no recovery is asked (dsg's state failed both).
  - status (additive): `info_send_errors` and `info_send_recoveries` beside `uncounted` (the info sends that failed, and the interface re-namings and reopens they asked).
- Consumers: nothing to port; dasio moves the stack version. API: `info::serve_info`, `serve_info_with` and `serve_shared` keep their signatures (a failed rebind at a reopen ends `serve_info_with` with the error); new public `info::sendwatch`; two new public fields `ctl::RequestCounts::{info_send_errors, info_send_recoveries}`, a source break only for code that builds `RequestCounts` by struct literal (such code adds the two fields). **No latency change.**

## 0.6.159

- **Feature (#354, obs#69): the engine pump's lost time split by cause, measured.** A late wake (`cause=late`, `behind_max_us`) or an overrun (`overruns`, `max_process_us`) said how much time the pump lost, not where. obs's strih soak failed twice on v0.6.155 with late wakes of 6-8.6 ms and three `cause=none` blocks of 1.7-2.3 ms, and whether dstack's own work (the socket drain before each block, #326; a converter's restart, #341) could cause either was only reasoned. Now the pump records, each as a peak since the start and that second's:
  - **the sleep's oversleep**: how late its sleep returned past the time it asked for (the media clock read right after the sleep less its target, when the sleep was asked before it, on one clock epoch). Near the wake's lateness: the scheduler ran the thread late.
  - **each drain's own time and datagrams** (the socket drain before each block in the normal profile; every datagram read, placed or not).
  - **each block's thread CPU time** (Linux, `CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID`; unmeasured elsewhere: Windows counts a thread's time per scheduler tick). A block far longer than its CPU time was preempted in its middle, not slow.
  - Status (`engine`, additive): `oversleep_max_us`, `drain_max_us`, `drain_max_datagrams`, `max_process_cpu_us` (absent where unmeasured). The pump watch's warning (`engine pump lost or overran blocks this second`, text unchanged) adds that second's `oversleep_max_us`, `drain_max_us`, `drain_max_datagrams` and `max_process_cpu_us` (`-` where unmeasured) after `behind_max_us`.
  - API (additive, dstack): `stream::split` (`Peak`, `oversleep_ns`, `cpu_between`, `thread_cpu_ns`, `timespec_ns`), `stream::SecondSplit`, `PumpStats::slept` (the pump thread calls it after each sleep), `Intake::datagrams`, `PumpView::oversleep_max_us`, `drain_max_us`, `drain_max_datagrams`, `max_process_cpu_us`. A consumer that builds a `PumpView` with a struct literal adds `..Default::default()`.
  - Cost: two `Instant` reads a drain; two thread CPU clock reads a block on Linux, each a system call (`CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID` has no vDSO path), made around `Pump::block`, never inside it; one media clock read before and one after each sleep, and one epoch read. No allocation, no lock.
- **Measured (#354): no dstack change since v0.6.148 makes a block or the drain slower.** On obs's shape (dlink `Rated`, 48 kHz app on a 96 kHz device, block 128, P 803, W 576, 1 RX and 2 TX channels, dante-input's sink and TX player) on dstack's pump draining a real loopback flow, v0.6.148, v0.6.155 and this release: the drain grows about 2 us a datagram (34 datagrams after a 10 ms stall: 71-86 us, the same in all three); the block after a re-bootstrap takes 47-52 us at most (#341's restart adds no silent block at 48 on 96 kHz; #343's arithmetic skip leaves nothing to remove there). Every block over 300 us of wall time had 15-113 us of thread CPU time, and under `chrt -f 10` no block passed 88 us in any version: obs's 1.7-2.3 ms `cause=none` blocks were the pump thread off the CPU, as its late wakes were. Figures: #354.
- **Test (#339): `soak_real_time`'s verdict reads a held write at a late tick as the runner's.** v0.6.158's tag CI (windows-2025) read OURS for a TX underrun at a tick 3.55 ms late whose write landed 10.99 ms after its depth read: the depth before the write (8.88 ms) was under the run's usual 14.85 ms less a call, so the rule refused the app as the cause. That depth was low because the tick itself was late (the pump took that time's frames on time). The usual depth of a tick is now the run's median less the lateness of its depth read (the tick's lateness and the time to that read) past the run's median one (`verdict::usual_tx_depth_ms`); with CI's other ticks 0.13-0.48 ms late the case reads RUNNER (it would up to a median lateness of 1.25 ms); the pump's backlog at the read never lowers it (a pump behind leaves the FIFO fuller) and stays in the dry point. A TX write dropped in `Fifo::push_with` still reads OURS (dev1, `chrt -f 10`, 2 of 2). Pass/fail unchanged.
- **Test (#351 follow-up): the `clock::linked` handover tests time their switch rounds.** v0.6.158's main CI (windows-2025) failed `the_linked_clock_waits_for_a_settled_follower_while_the_service_is_ready` (Service where Internal was due). Those tests assume the selector's three reads of a switch round (the service, the follower on the service's clock, the service) fall within the round's offset bound (30 µs to agree); a test thread not run between them reads the gap as an offset, which the selector counts against agreement by design (`offset_of`). Not a stack fault: on Linux, where #351's wait code never runs, v0.6.157 and v0.6.159 both fail these tests at dev1 load 70-90 (1 and 2 runs of 300). That #351 raised the Windows rate is probable, not measured: the binary's own TIME_CRITICAL tests pinned to CPU 0 now give that CPU away once per 10 ms instead of at every spin turn (#351's design), so a test thread queued there can wait longer; Windows CI showed no `clock::linked` failure from v0.6.152 to v0.6.157 and this one at v0.6.158. A run with a round over its bound (`AGREE_NS` 30 µs, `DRIFT_NS` 1 ms for a follower drifting 1 ms a round) that fails is run again (`clock::linked::tests::rounds::on_time`, up to 20 runs); a run with every round on time must pass every assertion as written (a handover bound of 0 still fails 11 tests at once).
- **No latency change** for any device or dlink app.
- Consumers: move the stack version; a status or log reader may show the pump's four new fields.

## 0.6.158

- **Fix (#351, dasio#120): a Windows media spin no longer yields in the last 500 µs before its due time.** The low-latency wait's spin called `SwitchToThread` on every turn up to the due time, and SwitchToThread hands the CPU to any ready thread on it, lower priority included, for the rest of that thread's slice. On dsg a process start had Windows Defender (priority 6-8) keep the pump's CPU 1416, 682 and 381 µs within 3 ms; a pump woken more than its W late re-bootstraps (dsg: W 96 at 96 kHz, 1 ms), so the pump re-bootstrapped (`cause=late`) about 10 times an hour. Now (`clock::wait::until`):
  - a spin turn with 500 µs or less to go pauses (`PAUSE`) and never yields, so a yield's victim makes the wake late by its slice less 500 µs at most: up to W + 500 µs (1.5 ms at dsg, the trace's 1416 µs included) a pump loses nothing. Before those 500 µs the spin yields on every turn, as before. dsg's pump (333 µs a period) waits less than 500 µs every time, so it no longer yields in its waits at all, except as below.
  - a hard cap keeps lower priorities running: a spun wait that would yield nothing (it starts inside the 500 µs) and would end 10 ms or more after the thread last gave its CPU away (a yield or any timer block, counted on the app's clock) gives once at its first turn: one yield, or, for an engine pump whose W absorbs the timer's late wake (`clock::pump_gives_on_timer`: W of at least `TIMER_LATE_NS`, 1.14 ms), the timer for the rest of the wait (its expiry preempts the lower priority at once). No spun wait outside the bounded escape goes more than 10 ms (and one pass of the thread's own work) without a give; the no-yield spins of v0.6.28 and v0.6.35 that froze FOH gave none, ever. On dsg, Defender can take the pump's CPU only at that capped yield, once per 10 ms instead of in every 333 µs period, so the late re-bootstraps should drop to about a third of today's, not to zero; the PC-side layer for long Defender scans stays dasio's (CPU sets).
  - Unchanged: the timer wait (`normal` profile), the bounded escape's spin (`spin_escape_ms`, it pauses already, uncapped), a wait already past its due time (no turn, no give, as before), Linux (never spins).
  - API (additive, dstack): `clock::pump_gives_on_timer(margin, rate)`, `clock::pump_give_line(margin, rate)` (the pump logs it at start on the Windows low-latency wait) and `clock::give_on_timer(bool)` (the pump thread sets it at start; a no-op on Linux).
- **Rollout:** this changes the scheduling of every Windows low-latency media thread. It ships to dasio's PCs (FOH, mbc, dsg) only after a soak on a non-production Windows box with pinned threads (resolume-snv) shows 0 freezes; the soak's box, hours and late wakes are recorded on #351.
- Consumers: nothing to port. dasio moves the stack version only after the soak above.
- **Tests (#339): `peer.rs`'s verdict now witnesses the sender's own threads.** Its witness thread (1 ms sleeps on the app's clock) saw only stalls of the whole process, so a run whose TX thread was not run while the witness was (dev1 at load ~20-45: a TX schedule restart with no witnessed stall) read `OURS`. Each pass now also reads both peers' TX lane: a TX silence while the witness ran (its packets flat; a lower bound that never holds the witness's own gap) counts only when the lane's own late record rose by its end (packets sent 5 ms or more past due, or a schedule restart; a silence still open when the witness stops is closed by its last pass), so a TX thread that ran on time and sent nothing (a stack fault dropping packets) is no evidence. The witness also merges each pump late wake as it comes (the status keeps the last 32 only; a pump not run, before or inside a block, wakes late at its next block), records `writes_after_send` rises (#346, now a counted cause beside the TX holes and restarts), and counts a ring-hole rise as a loss beside a tone-gap rise (a hole the tone check does not count still fails the test). Evidence at a loss: the witness's stall (6 ms), the sender's TX silence (8.08 ms: the 10 ms latency less fpp, the TX margin and a pass) or either pump's late wake (5 ms); it began by the loss's count and ended at most 60 ms before it (a TX thread still stalled when the receiver counts the loss counts too). The asserts are unchanged: a run that lost audio still fails; only the verdict text changed. Proven on dev1 under `chrt -f 10` (the test process real-time, so the box's load of 40-65 starved none of it): a temporary 25 ms sleep of one TX thread read `RUNNER` (its only evidence the TX silence, 25.4-27.4 ms; no witness stall), a temporary drop of the packets due in 8 ms of every 100 ms read `OURS` (every one of 213-249 losses without evidence), and the unchanged test passed. Test-only; no stack, API or latency change.
- **Tests (#339): the real-time soak (`dlink/tests/soak_real_time.rs`) names the thread that lost the audio.** Windows CI failed it twice (runs 43956272112, 43961897282) with one TX underrun at a tick only 0.6 / 1.1 ms late, and the message could not tell the box from a stack fault. Its depth before the write (11.9 / 13.5 ms) was the usual one, and the pump runs on the app's clock, so it never takes frames ahead of real time: the FIFO ran dry after the tick's lateness was read and before its write landed, so the test's own app thread was held there, which the lateness (read at the tick's start) never saw. Every failure of the loss rules now prints a verdict over each loss they refuse: `RUNNER` when each had a thread the box did not run long enough to lose it, else `OURS`. A TX underrun is the app's when its write landed (`Fifo::last_write`) past the dry point of the depth before it (that depth less one converter call, plus how far the pump was behind at the read), and only when that depth was the run's usual one. An RX dropout is the pump's with a late wake long enough or a restart, unless the RX FIFO held the whole read before it; a TX re-centre with a late wake long enough (a restart resyncs the TX player instead). The pump's late wakes are merged as they come. The message shows the pump's late wakes, overruns, `writes_after_send` and blocks over two periods at each loss. Pass rules unchanged: a run that lost audio still fails. Proven on dev1 under `chrt -f 10` with temporary changes, all reverted: the app thread held 16 ms between the depth read and the write reproduces CI's shape (a TX underrun at a tick 8 us late) and reads `RUNNER`; a TX write dropped in `Fifo::push_with` reads `OURS`; a pump stalled 8 ms loses nothing, 15 ms restarts it (allowed), and with the restart allowance taken out its RX dropout reads `RUNNER` (the pump not run 13.9 ms). Neither thread can be made to ride out such a stall without another latency: the app writes into a FIFO whose target is the device's app buffer (#247), and a pump past its W restarts.

- **Fix (#343, follow-up of #341): `dlink::Rated` keeps the app's timeline exact across a re-bootstrap at every rate pair and every start.** #341 reached a whole app frame through up to 40 silent blocks (`MAX_LEAD`), so a 48 kHz family app on a 44.1, 88.2 or 176.4 kHz device, 22.05 kHz on 192 kHz at 16-frame blocks and 11.025 kHz apps kept the old label: an impulse that crossed a pump stall came back up to one app sample off (measured on v0.6.156: -0.07 to -0.89 app frames at 48 kHz on 44.1 kHz, up to -0.97 at 48 kHz on 88.2 kHz, -0.50 at 11.025 kHz on 192 kHz). Now a restart puts both converters, both FIFOs and the next app label where a `Rated` that ran silence from device frame 0 would have them at that start, from closed forms of the converters' frame counts: no silent `Rated` blocks, no anchor search, no block grid, no cap. The converters step their phase with `dstack_resample::Converter::skip_silence` (below), which runs only the rest of one ratio period through the core with no channel active. Cost per restart, both converters, mean over the starts of two periods (dev1 at load ~41, release): 48 kHz on 44.1 kHz at 128 frames 4.6 us with 2 channels, 21 us with 64; 44.1 kHz on 96 kHz at 128 frames 4.5 / 48 us; 22.05 kHz on 192 kHz at 16 frames 43 us with 2 channels; 11.025 kHz on 192 kHz at 16 frames 80 us with 2 channels, 1.1 ms with 64 (`ceil((start mod period) / block)` core calls per converter, up to 160 there, and the core moves each channel's 512-sample history per call). The restart runs inside the restart's block, so at that last pair a restart can take one to 13 of its 83 us block periods; dlink needs no low latency and a restart follows a late block anyway, and #341 never reached a whole frame there at all. #341's silent block cost about 4 us each at 2 channels. The first label of a start that is not a whole app frame is now the RX converter's frames for that start (`floor((start - 2) * app / device)` for the sinc: 458, not 459, for device frame 1000 at 44.1 kHz on 96 kHz), the same label a `Rated` running from frame 0 gives; a fixed app block's calls now fall where such a run's do.
  - No latency change: `Rated::delay_ns`, `dlink::delay_ns` and `dlink::split` are unchanged (same converters, same prime).
  - API: dlink none. dstack-resample (changed, only dlink calls it): `Converter::skip_silence(frames, input, output) -> Result<(u64, u64), _>` puts a new or just-reset converter at the nominal ratio where `frames` frames of silence on its fixed side would (any split), and returns the input and output frames in all; `input`/`output` are scratch sized as for `process` at its largest. v0.6.154's form (one call's silence) is gone. New `Converter::period()`: the ratio's fewest whole frames on both sides. A skip after `set_ppm` is refused until a reset.

- Consumers: obs, camera-box, intercom, songplayer, presenter, iemmixer, dlisten, dsend and dasio move the stack version; nothing to port (a `PumpView` struct literal adds `..Default::default()`). obs's soak may print the new warning fields beside its verdict.

## 0.6.157

- **Fix (#352, dcontroller#101): a device latency write now reaches the RX flows already running.** A write over ARC (0x1101, Dante Controller's Device Config > Latency, `control::set_latency`, dtray) was taken and read back (`control::settings`: configured = active = the new value), but each running RX flow kept the latency it was made with: the flow read (0x3200) stated the old value, and a LOWER latency never reached the playout (seen on a dasio device: 40 to 5 ms, six flows stated 40000 us and played at 40 ms for more than 200 s; a higher latency did move the playout, but the flow read still stated the old one). Now `subscribe::Manager::set_latency` gives every running flow, unicast and multicast, the latency a new flow would get: its transmitter's advertised latency or the device's, the larger. The flow read states it at once, and the RX thread plays at it from the next manager tick, with the same flow (no ring restart). Nothing is sent to any transmitter (a Dante flow request carries no latency; a transmitter reads the receiver's settings) and no flow is asked for again, so a multicast flow is never written to.
  - The audio gap of a change, at the next manager tick: the device's playout point moves, as it did before for any write. The engine pump starts its schedule again once (`rebootstraps_retime` +1, `Cause::Retime`; already so before this fix). Under a dlink pull, a lower latency reads the skipped span in one burst (a restart, `restarts` +1, past its `span`), and a higher one waits that long with nothing written (a gap of the change's length). What is new: a lower write now really moves the flows' read point (before, the delay kept it at the old latency). Right after a lowering, packets count late against the new, lower playout. A flow whose transmitter advertises more than the new latency stays at the transmitter's, as for a new flow; a `playout_override` flow keeps playing at its override (at least the device latency), while the flow read states the rule's value, as before.
  - No API change. No default changes: the latency is the writer's value. **No latency change** for a device or dlink app that is not written to.
- **Tests (#349): a CI gate on what Dante Controller and dcontroller read from our devices.** dstack #345 (a heartbeat peaks record whose length left out its padding, so readers lost the lateness records of every device with a TX+RX count not a multiple of 4) passed CI: the tests used aligned channel counts and read the bytes back with our own `latency::parse`, which shared the writer's assumption. Now a strict walker written from hardware captures (`crates/dstack/tests/conformance/walk.rs`, test-only, calls no dstack reader) reads our heartbeat for every TX and RX count from 0 to 128, with and without a clock, our info replies (framing; the network info and clock stats compared with an UltimoX2's byte for byte outside ids and per-port counts) and channel-change notification (single channels 0 to 128, odd mask lengths; its mask rule is inferno's, as no hardware notification is captured yet), and the whole-device heartbeats of a running device with 0+2 and 3+2 channels heard on 224.0.0.233:8708; ARC 2.8.9 records are walked by their segment lengths with odd channel counts, odd name lengths and odd RX flow mappings. The hardware fixture (`tests/fixtures/heartbeat_hardware.json`: an UltimoX2 0+2 and 2+2, a Brooklyn card, a Yamaha card, and a dstack 0.6.150 heartbeat as a refused case; ids and addresses masked) proves the walker against real devices. With #345's fix reverted, the new unit tests and both whole-device tests fail. The walk found no other malformed packet.
- **Fix (#350): the heartbeat's lateness, the judge and the mean age report a packet's age at its ARRIVAL again, on every ring writer.** Since #326/#307 a packet waits in its socket until its writer reads it: an app's pull (dlisten's 10 ms output callback, dstack-asio's ASIO switch), the pump's block, or the RX thread's wake. The writer took the age at that read, so 0x8003 (the heartbeat's peak lateness), `lateness_peak`, `lateness_min_ns` (the judge) and #342's mean included the wait: dcontroller showed about 2.1 ms on an ASIO host's flow (0.4 ms before) and 10 to 11 ms on the dlistens, which Dante hardware and Dante Controller read as network latency. Now each placed packet's age is its arrival's:
  - Linux: exact, the age at the read less the kernel's receive stamp to the read (`SO_TIMESTAMPNS`, already read with every datagram).
  - Windows (no receive stamp): bracketed by the writer's reads. A packet read now came after the slot's last read that left its socket empty, and the packets of one read share one network delay, bounded by the newest packet's age at the read: each packet's arrival age is the larger of its age at the read less the time since that read, and the newest's. An on-time packet reads at most one packet interval above its true age (0.33 ms at 16 frames/48 kHz). A late packet reads at most one read period under: a burst that came late just before a read (a switch stalled 5 ms within a 10 ms pull) reads as the newest packet's age, so on Windows a late burst shows in the new `lateness_at_read_peak`, not in `lateness_peak`. The first read after the start or a flow change, and a read after one that left datagrams, failed, or read another socket (the move to a group's), keep the age at the read. With a kernel stamp whose wake exceeds the age (the wall clock stepped forward) the bracket decides instead.
  - What is judged at the read is unchanged: `late`, `late_at_read`, `wire_late`, holes and gaps (the reader's verdicts), the wake and write delays.
- **The wait is reported apart (status per RX flow, additive):** `lateness_at_read_peak` (the largest lateness at the read since the start, samples: `lateness_peak`'s figure before this release), `read_wait_peak_us` (since the start), `read_wait_mean_ns` (the heartbeat's last second; absent for a second without a placed packet), `read_wait_sum_ns` and `read_wait_count` (since the start, for a reader's own mean). A packet's age at the read is its arrival age plus its read wait. On Linux the read wait equals the wake delay.
  - API (additive, dstack): `RxCounts::lateness_at_read_peak`, `RxCounts::read_wait_peak_us`, `read_wait_sum_ns`, `read_wait_count`, `RxCounts::read_wait_mean_ns_since(&earlier)`, `RxShared::last_read_wait_mean_ns(slot)`. A consumer that builds an `RxCounts` with a struct literal adds `..Default::default()`.
- **No latency change** for any device or dlink app: P, (c), W and the app buffer are as in 0.6.156.
- Consumers:
  - #349: tests only; nothing to port.
  - #350: dasio's `lateness_mean_ns_since` and dcontroller's graph show the network figure again with no change; they may show `read_wait_*` beside it. obs, camera-box, intercom, songplayer, presenter, iemmixer, dlisten and dsend move the stack version; nothing to port.

## 0.6.156

- **Feature (#342, dasio#128): each RX flow slot's mean packet age, and its playout point, for an information line.** The owner wants dtray to show each RX flow's MEAN delivery latency next to its peaks, as information only. dstack kept only the least and the largest of a placed packet's age (our PTP now less its own stamp, the judge's `lateness_min_ns` figure), so no mean could be formed. Every ring writer now adds each placed packet's age to a running sum and count per slot: the RX thread's pass, the pump's drain and an app's drain, all in the one function they place through. This costs two relaxed `fetch_add` a packet, with no allocation and no lock. Two edge rules apply. A packet stamped after our now counts 0. An age past 4.29 s counts as 4.29 s, so the sum wraps only after 2^32 packets at that cap (16 days at 3000 packets a second). A reader diffs modulo 2^64; `lateness_mean_ns_since` does.
  - API (additive, dstack):
    - `RxCounts::lateness_sum_ns` and `RxCounts::lateness_count`: totals since the device started, like `lateness_peak`.
    - `RxCounts::lateness_mean_ns_since(&earlier) -> Option<u64>`: the mean, ns rounded down, of the packets placed between two looks; `None` when none was placed. Each reader keeps its own last look, so no reader takes another's window.
    - `RxShared::last_lateness_mean_ns(slot)`: the heartbeat's last second's mean, beside `last_lateness` and `last_min_lateness_ns`. Reading it takes nothing.
    - `RxShared::flow_playout(slot) -> Option<u32>`: the flow's published playout point (`RxPlan::latency`), samples. It is `None` without a flow, and for the moment a publish writes that slot.
    - A consumer that builds an `RxCounts` with a struct literal adds `..Default::default()`.
  - Status per RX flow (additive):
    - `lateness_mean_ns`: the last second's mean, ns. It is absent for a second without a placed packet, as `lateness_min_ns` is.
    - `lateness_sum_ns` and `lateness_count`: since the start. Diff two reads for the mean between them.
    - `playout` was already there.
- **Fix (#346): a pump block whose outputs come after the TX thread sent them now counts on the pump.** The pump judges only its wake against W; a block that woke within W but ended past the TX thread's slack (W + fpp + the TX margin from its due time: its work, a preemption) was written after the TX read its stamps, so the TX sent the block as silence (its lane's `holes`) while the pump counted no late wake, re-bootstrap or skipped block: a gap on the receivers that the pump's own counters did not show (the likely cause of #339's `peer.rs` case 1). The engine ring now tells its writer: `Ring::put_at` stores with an atomic swap and returns how many of its positions the reader had already read as holes (decided by the same compare-and-swap the reader marks them with, so exact both ways); the pump counts each block with any such sample, whatever the cause (also the first block after a lower device latency or a re-anchor, when it lands on stamps already sent; then beside that re-bootstrap). It shows as `PumpView::writes_after_send`, the engine status key `engine.writes_after_send`, and in the pump watch's warning (`engine pump lost or overran blocks this second`, new field `writes_after_send`; a second where only it grew now warns too, with `cause=none`). The ring's counters (`written`, `holes`, `late`, ...) are unchanged, and so is what is sent.
  - API (additive, dstack): `PumpView::writes_after_send`, `watch::Grew::writes_after_send` (a consumer building a `Grew` or `PumpView` with a struct literal adds it or uses `..Default::default()`), the status key. API change (dstack): `media::ring::Ring::put_at` returns `usize` (was `()`); a caller that ignores it compiles as before.
  - The pump's real-time part gains one atomic swap per output sample (was a store) and a relaxed add on such a block: no allocation, lock or system call.
- **No latency change** for any device or dlink app: P, (c), W and the app buffer are as in 0.6.155. Nothing reads the new figures to decide anything.
- Consumers:
  - dasio keeps each slot's `RxCounts` from its 10 s look. It shows `lateness_mean_ns_since(&previous)` next to its peaks (`lateness_peak`, `last_lateness`, `last_min_lateness_ns`), and `flow_playout(slot)` as the latency the age must stay under. It still never calls `take_max_lateness`.
  - obs, camera-box, intercom, songplayer, presenter, iemmixer, dlisten and dsend move the stack version; nothing to port.
  - #346: nothing to port. A status reader may show `engine.writes_after_send` beside the TX lane's `holes`.

## 0.6.155

- **Fix (#307, step 2: dstack-asio's Dante -> ASIO side): an ASIO bridge's output no longer depends on the pump's lateness.** The pump wrote each RX block into the to-ASIO FIFO (`DanteSide`) and the driver's buffer switch only played it (`AsioSide`), so a pump that woke late let the FIFO (the ASIO buffer + one pump block: 4 ms at 96 kHz by default) run dry although the audio already waited in the RX rings or the flow sockets: dlisten's class before 0.6.153. Now, as there, the thread with the deadline does the work: once the app hands the bridge its `RxPull`, each buffer switch first drains the flow sockets into the RX rings and reads them by position (media now less P) into the FIFO in one push, then plays through the player as before; the pump writes nothing there. A whole-device test (`crates/dstack-asio/tests/to_asio_pull.rs`: the pump stalled 10 to 20 ms at random, 31 stalls, against a steady 256-frame driver period at 48 kHz) had 13 to-ASIO underruns and 6350 samples off the level in 8 s with the pump's thread filling the FIFO (its RED commit: the same read by position, made on the pump's thread; 0.6.153's block feed cannot run under a pulling device, whose pump has no RX inputs); it now has 0 and 0, and no packet counted late.
  - API (additive, dstack-asio): `Bridge::pull_to_asio(RxPull)` (refused for a bridge with no to-ASIO channels, a pull of another FIFO than `Bridge::to_asio_fifo`, a pull after the driver's first open, or a second pull), `Bridge::keep_pulled() -> Option<(Look, bool)>` (the app's look about every 250 ms: its period must exceed the driver's buffer period, under 150 ms at the default target, or a running driver reads as stopped; while no driver side pulls, no driver open, it drains the flow itself, `RxPull::drain_idle`, so the flow stays heard; it never waits for the lock; the `bool` says it cleared a lock a panicking pull poisoned), `Bridge::to_asio_pull_stats`, `bridge::to_asio_chunk_ns`. Pulled, the to-ASIO player's producer chunk is the ASIO buffer (the switch writes it once a call) instead of the pump block. A pump block with no RX inputs (a pulling app's pump) never feeds the to-ASIO FIFO. Without `pull_to_asio` the bridge runs as in 0.6.153.
  - API (additive, dlink): `bridge::SharedPull` (re-exported as `bridge::pull::SharedPull` too): one `RxPull` shared by the consumer's callbacks and the watching thread: `pull` (only tries the lock; a held lock pulls nothing, a poisoned one pulls on), `keep(&mut IdleWatch) -> Look` (the idle drain, under the lock only while no callback pulled since the look), `clear_poison`, `stats`, `fifo`, `channels`. dlisten and dstack-asio both run it (dlisten's output callback and main thread had their own copy of the same logic; the same behaviour and log lines, the idle drain now runs just before them). `RxPull::fifo` and `RxPull::channels` (what it writes and reads).
  - API change (dlink): **`RxPull::new` returns `Result<RxPull, String>`**: it refuses a FIFO without one channel per RX channel read (its first read would index past its scratch, a panic on the consumer's thread) and an RX channel past the device's (`bridge::pull::check`, the rule `Link::start_pulling` already applied before its start). A caller of `RxPull::new` handles the `Err(String)` (`?` where its error type takes a `String`, else e.g. `.map_err(anyhow::Error::msg)?`). `Config::bridge_buffer`'s doc names the producer chunk of a pulled FIFO (the app's callback).
  - The driver's switch never waits on a lock (`SharedPull::pull`: only an earlier run's last switch or the idle drain can hold it, and the switch then plays what the FIFO holds, once maybe a little emptier) and allocates nothing (`tests/to_asio_alloc.rs`); its part after the pull runs under RealtimeSanitizer in CI (`crates/dstack-asio/tests/rtsan.rs`, a new step of the `rtsan` job). Its system calls are the pull's: the clock read and the sockets' non-blocking reads.
  - #307 step 1's open item, a start position for `fill_at`: not needed (the driver's `Engine` gives no block start, the pulled reads are contiguous, and a gap past the FIFO is the pull's counted restart).
- **Tests (#339): two windows-2025 whole-device failures traced; each test now names the thread that lost the audio.** No stack defect: v0.6.153 changed nothing on either path. `tests/rx_stall.rs` lost 1920 samples in the normal profile, all written after their read: under the pump as the RX writer a hole means the packet was not in the socket at the drain before its read, so the test's own sender sent it late (a 35 ms sender stall on dev1 gives the same signature). Its samples now encode their position, and a failure says `SENDER` (every hole in a packet sent at or after its earliest read, with the sender loop's largest gap) or `OURS`, with the pump's view and late wakes. `tests/peer.rs` heard one gap with every receiving counter at 0: the gap was in what the sender sent, whose status the message did not print. `Counts` now reads the sender's TX `holes` (a pump block that wakes within W but ends past it leaves only these) and the ring's `late`, a witness thread records the runner's stalls on the app clock, and a failure prints both peers' statuses with a `RUNNER` or `OURS` verdict. The pass rules are unchanged: a run where the runner did not run a thread still fails.
- **Fix (#345, dasio's question of 16.11.2026: no latency graph for mbc-snv in dcontroller): a heartbeat's latency records read whatever the channel count.** Our heartbeat wrote a record's length and content length without its padding to 4 bytes, so a reader that walks to the next record by the length (dcontroller, Dante Controller's Latency tab, our own `latency::parse`) lost the lateness (0x8003) and late count (0x8004) records behind a peaks record (0x8002) whose TX + RX channel count is not a multiple of 4: on the network every dlisten and dsend (2 channels) and mbc-snv (32 + 54). Both lengths now count the padding, as the hardware's do (an UltimoX2 with 2 RX channels writes `00 1c 80 02 00 04 00 10`). No API change.
- **Tests (#339): `tests/rx_stall.rs`'s own sender survives a runner stall.** Its sender sent each packet 4 ms ahead of its media time, so a shared runner that left the sender thread unrun for 16-35 ms (windows-2025, three failures on 16.11.2026) delivered packets after their read and the run tested nothing. It now sends 48 ms ahead (the ring stores up to 65536 samples ahead of its reader; both stalled tests assert `early` 0), so a sender stall up to about 57 ms loses nothing, and the RX thread's stall rose from 20 to 70 ms, still past the time a packet waits in its socket before its read, so the low-latency control still loses samples. The pass rules are unchanged; the normal-profile test now catches only an RX writer later than about 57 ms (was 13 ms), which the 70 ms stall still is. On dev1: a temporary 35 ms sender sleep no longer fails the normal-profile test (a 70 ms one fails it with `SENDER`, 704 samples); a temporary RX-path fault (the pump not draining, the RX thread writing as before 0.6.148) fails it with `OURS`, 3888 samples. `tests/peer.rs` is unchanged: its sender is a dstack device on the stack's own TX schedule, which a test cannot move ahead. No stack or API change, no latency change.
- **No latency change** for any device or dlink app: P, (c), W and the app buffer (the bridge target) are as in 0.6.153. The to-ASIO target stays the ASIO buffer + one pump block (8 ms at 256 frames, 48 kHz). Measured on dev1 at that target without stalls, the mean wait from a sample's due time (position + P) to its playing: 9.32 ms pulled (`to_asio_pull.rs`, three runs, 9.31-9.32 ms; it bounds the wait under the target + one block), against 10.70 ms written by the pump (a throwaway copy of the same test on the unpulled path with the stalls off: 9.37 ms in the FIFO + half a pump block, 1.33 ms, in the ring; it also had 1 underrun in one of two runs without any stall).
- Consumers: dasio ports its bridge instance (`run_bridge.rs`) to the pull: claim `media.rx.app_intake()` before `Pump::new`, give the pump a map with no RX channels (`rx: vec![]`), build `RxPull::new(intake, media.clock, media.rx, (0..to_asio).collect(), bridge.to_asio_fifo(), timing.playout, cfg.sample_rate)` (an `Err(String)` when it does not fit), hand it over with `bridge.pull_to_asio(pull)` before `spawn_runner`, and call `bridge.keep_pulled()` from its loop every 250 ms (an `Every` of its own: a look every 100 ms pass would read a running driver with a buffer over 100 ms as stopped and drain under it) and log a `look.changed` both ways, a `restarts_rose` and a cleared poison; add `to_asio_pull_stats()`' `restarts` to its status if wanted. The driver's thread now makes the pull's non-blocking socket reads (still no child process, no allocation, no lock wait). dlisten moves with the workspace. obs, camera-box, intercom, songplayer, presenter, iemmixer, dsend: move the stack version; nothing to port.

## 0.6.154

- **Fix (#341, dasio#152): `dlink::Rated` keeps the app's timeline exact across a pump re-bootstrap.** At a ratio that does not divide the device block (44.1 kHz on 96 kHz) a re-bootstrap restarted both converters at the new device start `s` but labelled the app's first frame `floor(s * 44100 / 96000)`, so every app frame of the new run was labelled up to one app sample early, by a fraction that differed at each restart. An impulse the app sent before a pump stall and heard after it came back shifted by that difference: dasio's e2e read [723, 722, 723] samples after a 9.2 ms late pump wake. Now a restart (the first block, and the first after each discontinuity) starts the converters at the latest earlier block start that is a whole app frame (on the 128-frame grid at most 4 blocks back), through silent blocks that call no app and send nothing; the app's frames are then labelled exactly, on `Rated::new` and `Rated::with_fixed_app_block` alike. The silent blocks cost no filter work (the converter steps its phase with no channel active): about 4 us each with 2 channels, 7 us with 16 (dev1, release). A restart runs at most 40 (`MAX_LEAD`, at most about 0.3 ms), which covers every app from 16 to 192 kHz on a 48 or 96 kHz device at every block size, and on a 192 kHz one but 22.05 kHz at 16 frames. Longer periods keep the old label: an app of the 48 kHz family on a 44.1, 88.2 or 176.4 kHz device (up to 146 or 440 blocks would be needed), 22.05 kHz on 192 kHz at 16 frames, and 11.025 kHz. So does a start off the block grid (the pump never makes one). 48 kHz on 96 kHz (2:1) and one rate never needed it and are unchanged. The label is integer math now (was `f64`).
  - Test: a device whose output loops back to its input re-bootstraps while an impulse is in the network, at five new starts covering every app phase of the 128-frame grid, on both paths: the round trip stays the same to a hundredth of a sample (`rated::tests::rebootstrap`); a restarted `Rated::new`'s app blocks carry the media starts and frames of one that ran from frame 0, with no RX or no TX channel too; `tests/rated_alloc.rs` now starts off a whole app frame, so its restarts run silent blocks under the allocation check. Before the fix it moved (0.16 sample at the first new start the test tries).
  - API: dlink none. dstack-resample (additive): `Converter::skip_silence`, a call of silence with no filter work and nothing written, valid only while the converter's history is silent (new or just reset; an error otherwise).
- **No latency change** for any device or dlink app: P, (c), W, the app buffer and `Rated::delay_ns` / `dlink::delay_ns` are as in 0.6.153. Only the sub-sample placement of an app's frames after a restart moves (by under one app sample), which is the fix.
- Consumers: dasio moves the tag and adds the same round-trip check across a re-bootstrap on its `Served` path. obs, camera-box, intercom, songplayer, presenter, iemmixer, dlisten/dsend: move the stack version; nothing to port.

## 0.6.153

- **Fix (#307, step 1: dlisten): an RX bridge whose consumer has its own deadline no longer depends on the pump's lateness.** dlisten's pump wrote each block into the bridge FIFO and the output callback only read it, so a pump that woke late (2 to 8 ms on a loaded PC, a whole-PC stall) let the FIFO run dry although the audio already waited in the RX rings or the flow sockets (field: 4 bridge underruns on FOH since 14.11, 3142 on mbc before its CPU move). The owner rejected covering it with a latency term (+2 ms, 16.11.2026). Now the thread with the deadline does the work: at each callback dlisten's output drains the flow sockets into the RX rings itself and reads them by sample position (media now less the device's latency P, each channel at its flow's delay) into the FIFO in one push, then plays through the player as before. The pump is out of the bridge's path. A whole-device test (`crates/dlink/tests/rx_pull.rs`: the pump stalled 10 to 20 ms at random, about 60 stalls a run, against a steady 10 ms output) had 10 bridge underruns and 9020 samples off the level with the pump writing the FIFO; it now has 0 and 0, and no packet counted late.
  - API (additive, dlink): `Link::start_pulling` / `Link::start_pulling_on` (the pump gets no RX inputs; the app gets the rings' writer) return a `bridge::RxPull`: `pull` (call it at the top of each callback, before `Player::fill_with`), `drain_idle` (from another thread while the consumer does not pull: it reads what waited until the sockets are empty, counted `unplaced`, never placed), `new`, `stats` (`PullStats::pulls`, `restarts`, `frames`, `idle_drains`); `bridge::pull::{due, check, reach, Due, IdleWatch, Look, IDLE_PASSES}` (`IdleWatch` tells the watching thread when the consumer stopped and came back, and the restarts' rise). A pull reads at most the FIFO's capacity and half a ring (`reach`), else starts again at now. `pull` allocates nothing and takes no lock (dlisten shares it between output streams through a `Mutex::try_lock` that never waits) (`tests/rx_pull_alloc.rs`); its ring read and FIFO write, and its drain's packet placing, run under RealtimeSanitizer in CI (`tests/rtsan.rs`, a new step of the `rtsan` job).
  - API (additive, dstack): `media::rx::RxShared::app_intake` and `app_writes` (the app as the rings' third writer, beside the pump and the RX thread, dstack #326's single-writer rule: claimed before either), `embed::Embedded::start_pulling` and `Embedded::timing`. The RX thread's start log line gains `app_writes`. The ring-writer claims moved to `media/rx/claim.rs` (no change). `Intake::drain_into_rings` now returns whether it left every socket empty (`false`: a slot hit `PUMP_DRAIN`); a caller that ignores it compiles as before.
  - Counters under the app's writer: a packet is `late` (and `wire_late`; a hole lost) past P plus the time since the app's last drain (the reader has read exactly that far), not P plus the pump's block; the wake and write delays include the wait for the next callback. While an app drains idle, what it reads is counted `packets` and `unplaced`. The pump's and the RX thread's counting is unchanged.
  - A pulling app must pull from the device's start: the pull is the rings' only writer, so while nothing pulls the flow reads silent, no keepalive goes out, the channel stays `InProgress`, and a granted flow is closed and asked again. An app whose consumer may stop calls `drain_idle` meanwhile.
  - dlisten: its output callback pulls (the `feed` module and its `Feed` processor are removed; the pump runs an idle processor). Its main thread now ticks every 250 ms on a deadline (was a 1 s wake; the status log stays every 10 s, the output list every 5 s): while no output pulls (none open, a stream that stopped calling back) it drains the flow itself (`drain_idle`, only while no callback pulled since its look), so the flow stays alive and the audio returns at the next callback; it logs the change both ways, each rise of the pull's restarts, and a panicked pull. Its FIFO and player run at the device's rate (the same as its `--rate`, since dlisten sets no network rate), and the player's producer chunk is the output's period (the app buffer's chunk) instead of the pump block, since the callback now writes the FIFO.
- **No latency change** for any device or dlink app: P, (c), W and the app buffer (the bridge target) are as in 0.6.152. dlisten's bridge keeps its target (the output's 10 ms period + one pump block, 12.667 ms at 48 kHz). Measured on dev1 at that target, the mean wait from a sample's due time (position + P) to its playing: 14.96 ms written by the pump (13.63 ms in the FIFO + half a pump block in the ring), 14.34 ms pulled (`rx_pull.rs` bounds it under the target + one block).
- Consumers: dlisten moves with the workspace. dasio: the same pull for dstack-asio's to-ASIO side is #307's next step (not in this release); nothing to port yet. obs, camera-box, intercom, songplayer, presenter, iemmixer, dsend: move the stack version; nothing to port.

## 0.6.152

- **Test fix (#328, the v0.6.151 tag CI on windows-2025): the linked clock's handover tests no longer depend on the time between setting the fake follower and the selector's round.** The tests' fake follower held a fixed time (the service's, read when the test set it) while the service kept running, so a gap over the 30 us handover tolerance (a preempted test thread, or a wall-clock step, which a bare `MonoClock` reads on Windows before a poll) read as disagreement and failed `a_forced_switch_that_disagrees_re_aligns_at_once`. The fake now runs on the service's clock. No library change, no API change.
- **No latency change** for any device or dlink app: P, (c), W and the app buffer (the bridge target) are as in 0.6.151.
- Consumers: nothing to port; moving the stack version is optional.

## 0.6.151

- **Fix (#325, dasio's field reading of 16.11.2026): the ASIO bridge's receiver figure is the TX packets' measured age, not the modelled block age.** 0.6.149's `from_asio_min_receiver_latency_us` modelled the from-ASIO block age (call period + hand-over wait) + one pump block: 2222 us on a field PC (128 frames at 96 kHz) while the bridge's TX packets peaked at 1003-1397 us past their stamps, so its WARN would have named clean 2 ms receivers. Now the figure comes from the TX side's own measure of every packet it sends (its age past its first sample's media stamp, what a receiver judges):
  - Status keys (dasio's `bridge_json`, from `BlockAge::report()`): **new** `from_asio_packet_age_us` (the oldest TX packet past its stamp over the last minute); **changed** `from_asio_min_receiver_latency_us` = that + the network (the device's measured network latency p99, `RxShared::network_p99_ns`, or 200 us while none is measured: always on Windows); `from_asio_block_age_us` and `from_asio_block_age_max_us` stay as a diagnostic of the driver's calls (not a receiver threshold). Each key is absent while its value is unknown (the packet ones until the first TX packet, the block ones while no block was taken, each for one to two minutes after the last).
  - The once-an-hour WARN (`asio bridge: a receiver counted our TX packets late: <name> at <latency> us (<n> late); ...`) now fires only when a TX flow's own late count (`over_latency`, packets over the latency the receiver states, or our advertised one when higher) rose, naming the receiver, the latency it states and the late packets; a rise inside the hour is held and summed into the next warning, never lost. The WARN against the modelled threshold is removed.
  - API (changed, dstack-asio): `AgeReport` is `{ block_age_us, block_age_max_us, packet_age_us, min_receiver_latency_us }`, each an `Option<u64>`; `BlockAge::report()` is `None` only while neither a block nor a packet is known. `BlockAge::new(clock)` (no pump block). `BlockAge::look(&AppView, Option<u64>)` replaces `BlockAge::warn_low_receivers` and `low_receivers_text` (removed). New `dstack_asio::bridge::NETWORK_ALLOWANCE_NS` (200 us).
  - API (dstack): new `media::tx::{AppView, ReceiverLateness}`, `TxShared::take_app_view` (takes the oldest packet since the app's last view, a per-lane peak of its own: the late watch's `take_age_peak` is untouched), `RxShared::network_p99_ns`. Removed `TxShared::receiver_latencies` (its only caller was dasio's bridge loop; `take_app_view` carries each receiver's latency).
- **Fix (#335, dasio#107): `dlink::Rated` zeroes the app's outputs before each call, as the pump does.** `call_app` reused its output buffer, so an output the app left alone sent the previous block again (on `Rated::new` and `Rated::with_fixed_app_block`). It now plays silence there. No API change; dasio can drop its zeroing wrapper (`Zeroed`).
- **No latency change** for any device or dlink app: P, (c), W and the app buffer (the bridge target) are as in 0.6.150.
- Consumers: dasio drops its `Zeroed` wrapper around `Rated` (#335), and ports its bridge loop to `age.look(&media.tx.take_app_view(), media.rx.network_p99_ns())` (in place of `from_asio_age_tick`'s `warn_low_receivers(&media.tx.receiver_latencies())`; once a counts tick, never the pump: the view locks the TX table, and runs whether or not a driver serves), and `age_json` writes each present key, `from_asio_packet_age_us` new (told by peer). obs, camera-box, intercom, songplayer, presenter, iemmixer: move the stack version; nothing to port.

## 0.6.150

- **Fix (#332, dasio): a reader of the pump's counters never sees skipped blocks before their cause.** `PumpStats::rebootstrapped` counted `missed` before the cause, so a second reader (dasio's shm probe) could see blocks with no re-bootstrap cause. The cause is counted first and `missed` is stored with Release; `PumpStats::view` (and `stream_stats`) load `missed` first with Acquire. A reader of its own loads `missed` first too. No API change.
- **Feature (#330, asked by dasio): `dlink::Rated` can give the app a fixed block.** `Rated::with_fixed_app_block` (same arguments as `Rated::new`) calls the app with exactly `A = ceil(block * app_rate / device_rate)` frames every time, for a fixed-buffer app (an ASIO host); `Rated::app_block()` gives `A` (`None` for `Rated::new`). At a ratio that does not divide the block (44.1 kHz on 96 kHz) the RX converter's frames wait in a FIFO with no prime, and the app is called once in a device block that brings the FIFO to `A` or more, else not at all: 0 or 1 calls a device block, `app_rate / A` calls a second on average (44.1 kHz, block 128: `A` = 59, one device block in about 295 has no call), never a dry FIFO. The TX FIFO's prime grows from `PRIME` (8) to `A + PRIME` frames on that path. Each call's media start follows the app's own contiguous frames, and its play time sits as many frames before the device block's (P + (c)) as were waiting. At an exact 2:1 or 1:2 (48 kHz on 96 kHz, an even block) every device block gives exactly `A`: the path is `Rated::new`'s, sample for sample.
  - Tests: ten simulated minutes at 44.1 kHz on 96 kHz, block 128 (`crates/dlink/tests/rated_fixed.rs`, about 50 s unoptimised; 20 s of it in the unit tests): every call exactly 59 frames, at most one call a device block, neither FIFO dry, the call rate R / A within 1 ppm. An impulse and a rise sweep measure the delay each way against the plain path; `tests/rated_alloc.rs` proves `process` allocates nothing on both paths.
  - API (additive): `Rated::with_fixed_app_block`, `Rated::app_block`.
- **Latency:** no change for any existing dlink app (`Rated::new` and `Link` are as before). An app that opts in at an inexact ratio pays its FIFO, which `Rated::delay_ns` states: RX + up to (A - 1) / R, TX + A / R. At 44.1 kHz on 96 kHz, block 128: RX 1.32 -> 2.63 ms (+1.32 ms), TX 3.08 -> 4.42 ms (+1.34 ms). At 48 kHz on 96 kHz: no change (0.63 / 0.64 ms).
- Consumers: dasio can drop its own fixed-block adapter for `Rated::with_fixed_app_block` after the tag bump. obs, camera-box, intercom, songplayer, presenter, dlisten/dsend: move the stack version; nothing to port.

## 0.6.149

- **Feature (#325, dasio#120): the ASIO bridge measures its from-ASIO block age.** A driver hands a whole call's inputs at once, so the first sample of a block is already one call old at the hand-over (128 frames at 96 kHz: 1.333 ms), and it waits for the pump's next block on top. A receiver whose latency is under that age plus one pump block can get late packets from a bridge's TX (a 1 ms receiver against 128-frame calls at 96 kHz, dasio#120). The driver's side now records each hand-over (its call period, and its time while the pump has not taken an earlier one), and the pump's side the age of the oldest untaken hand-over when it takes the from-ASIO FIFO: on the app's clock, atomics only, no allocation or lock on either side.
  - New values for the bridge's status (dasio's `bridge_json` adds the keys): `Bridge::from_asio_age()` -> `BlockAge::report()` gives `AgeReport { block_age_us, block_age_max_us, min_receiver_latency_us }`, for `from_asio_block_age_us` (the last block), `from_asio_block_age_max_us` (the max over the last minute: one to two minutes, two windows of one) and `from_asio_min_receiver_latency_us` (that max + one pump block, the smallest receiver latency that never sees a late packet from this path). `None` before the first take and once no block was taken for one to two minutes (no driver yet, or a closed one). The age leaves out the from-ASIO FIFO's own depth (the bridge target).
  - A WARN at most once an hour when a TX flow's known receiver latency is below it: `BlockAge::warn_low_receivers(&media.tx.receiver_latencies())` from the app's status loop (never the pump), naming each such receiver and the latency it needs.
  - API (additive): `dstack_asio::bridge::{BlockAge, AgeReport, low_receivers_text}`, `Bridge::{from_asio_age, with_clock}`, `media::tx::TxShared::receiver_latencies`. dstack-asio now depends on dstack directly (already in every consumer's lockfile).
- **Fix (#328, the remaining line): the TEST system clock's waits sleep on the app's clock.** `SkewedClock::sleep_until` slept a wait computed on the app's clock on the media threads' monotonic clock, which on Windows is the wall until a poll (none runs on the test clock), so a wall step during a sleep moved the wake. It now sleeps to its target on the app's clock (the private `sleep_until_app`: raw QPC on Windows, `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` on Linux, unchanged there). The Windows wait loop (the timer to the spin tail, the yielding spin, the bounded escape) is now one cross-platform function over the clock it is given (`clock::wait::until`, tested on Linux with a fake clock), unchanged in what it does. The bounded escape's period bookkeeping (`ask_escape`, `defer_escape`, `block_escape`, the wait's take) reads the app's clock on every thread, so a thread's ask and its wait share one clock; the period is a cadence (100 ms by default when set), which raw QPC keeps without a step.
- **No latency change** for any device or dlink app: P, (c), W and the app buffer (the bridge target) are as in 0.6.148.
- Consumers: dasio adds the three `from_asio_*` status keys from `Bridge::from_asio_age().report()` and calls `warn_low_receivers` from its status loop (told by peer). obs, camera-box, intercom, songplayer, presenter, iemmixer: move the stack version; nothing to port.

## 0.6.148

- **Fix (#326, field strih-snv 15-16.11.2026): in the normal profile the engine's pump writes the RX rings itself, so a late helper thread no longer costs audio.** Under OBS's load the scheduler left the normal-priority RX thread behind by up to 17.5 ms, more than the 13 ms between a packet's arrival and the pump's read of its samples (P), and the thread then wrote them behind the reader: obs's `rx_holes` = `rx_written_late` in bursts (3773 by 01:01) while `rx_late` stayed 0. Now, in the normal profile (every dlink app), the pump drains the flow sockets itself, non-blocking, just before it reads each block, and writes the rings; the RX thread keeps the multicast joins, the keepalives and the statistics, and polls no flow socket (a wake at least every 33 ms plus up to 4 a second per granted flow for its keepalives, instead of up to 10 000 a second). The low-latency profile (iemmixer gen3) and a device without a pump (dstack-dev) are unchanged: the RX thread writes. The choice is made once at the start; the rings keep one writer.
  - Counters, same names and places: `late`, `wire_late`, `late_at_read`, `gaps` and the ring counts are judged at the read as before. Under the pump the wake delay (`wake_delay_*`, the minute line's `wake_delay_*_us`) and the write delay (`write_delay_*_us`) also include the wait for the next block: up to one block (2.7 ms for dlink's default 128 frames at 48 kHz) plus the pump's own lateness, where they were tens of µs. A datagram to a slot with no flow is still read and counted `stray` by the RX thread (under the pump at each of its wakes); a new flow's first drain also drops, as `stray`, what waited in its slot before it (with at most a block of the new flow's own first datagrams), so a re-patch never starts behind the old flow's backlog.
  - The test path: `tests/rx_stall.rs` stalls the RX thread 20 ms six times in 3 s past an 8 ms P (strih's shape): 3408 late samples and as many holes before, 0 now; the low-latency control still shows them.
  - API (additive): `media::rx::Intake` (`drain_into_rings`), `media::rx::PUMP_DRAIN`, `RxShared::{pump_intake, pump_writes}`, `stream::Pump::drains`, and `RxShared::stall_thread` (doc-hidden, tests only). The RX thread's start line `RX flow sockets' receive buffer` gains the field `pump_writes`.
- **Fix (#328): the TEST system clock runs on the app's clock on Windows too.** A device on `ClockSource::System` (`--clock system`, dlink's test clock, the CI peers) with no `test_clock_ppm` ran on `clock::monotonic_ns`, which on Windows is the wall clock until dsync's poller updates it, and no poller runs on that source: the device followed the runner's time service (steps and slew, 66 ppm off QPC on windows-2025, #305). `skewed::media_ptp` now always gives that source a `SkewedClock` on `clock::app_monotonic_ns` (raw QPC on Windows, `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` on Linux), at 0 ppm when no skew is set. Its waits still sleep on the media threads' clock, as the skewed clock's always did (a few ppm, and a wall step during a sleep before the first poll wakes it early or late by that step). Linux is unchanged (the same clock). Production clocks (dsync, linked) are untouched.
- **No latency change** for any device or dlink app: P, (c), W and the app buffer are as in 0.6.147.
- Consumers: a Windows test on the system clock gets a stable clock (#328). (obs, camera-box, intercom, songplayer, presenter, dlisten/dsend): move the stack version; nothing to port. obs: `rx_holes` and `rx_written_late` should stay flat on a loaded PC; the wake and write delay figures in its status now read up to a block higher.

## 0.6.147

- **Test fix (#304): the real-time soak case runs its pump on the app's clock.** `crates/dlink/tests/soak_real_time.rs` gave the pump a bare `MonoClock` that no poller ever fed; on Windows that reads the wall clock (`clock::monotonic_ns` before the steer model's first poll), which the CI runner's time service adjusts under the test, while the app's ticks and the TX FIFO's write stamps read raw QPC. A forward step of the pump's clock runs that much audio at once and the TX FIFO loses the depth, so main CI of 0.6.146 (and a dev run of 0.6.143) gapped at a tick only 2.2-2.7 ms late. The pump now runs on `SkewedClock` at 0 ppm on `clock::app_monotonic_ns`, the clock the ticks read; a gap failure also lists the five ticks before it with the TX FIFO's depth. No stack code changed.
- **No latency change** for any device or dlink app: P, (c), W and the app buffer are as in 0.6.146.
- Consumers: nothing to port; move the stack version when convenient.

## 0.6.146

- **Feature (#323, follows #321): a stale local client (dtray, dtray-cli) now shows on the network.** A client below `NETWORK_FLOOR` reached only the device's local status endpoint (127.0.0.1), which dcontroller cannot read: exactly #319's stale dtray beside a device. Now:
  - mDNS: while a local client below the floor asked within the hour, the device's `_netaudio-arc` TXT carries the new key `dstack_old_client=<the lowest such version>` (e.g. `dstack_old_client=0.6.20`), after `dstack_vers` and `dstack_ctl`. With none, the key is absent and the records are byte for byte as in 0.6.145. The daemon checks it every subscription tick (250 ms) and announces the ARC record again once each time the value changes (the client upgraded, or the hour since it last asked passed); no other record and no periodic traffic.
  - `discover::Discovery::below_floor` (and `network_floor::below_of`, so `warn_devices` and Discovery's own hourly WARN) now also lists each device that announces the key, as `kind` `client`, `name` the device's Dante name, `address` its ARC address, `version` the client's. A value that does not read, or is not below the reader's floor, names nothing.
  - API (changed): `daemon::announce_device` takes a fourth argument, `old_client: Option<network_floor::Version>` (`None` = as before). API (additive): `announce::OLD_CLIENT_KEY`, `announce::with_old_client`, `network_floor::Clients::lowest_below_floor`, `Media::old_client`. `Below::name`/`address` now also describe a client a device announces.
- **Fix (#319, field 16.11.2026): the 96 s browser alarm no longer names Dante hardware.** dcontroller's relay alarmed on an Audinate-based interface: Audinate firmware refreshes its own mDNS cache every 96 s, which is normal Dante behaviour. The meter now never names a source that announced `_netaudio-arc` records without `dstack_vers` within the last hour (and none with it): such a source is Dante hardware. It reads those records from the answers it already parses for the network floor. A PC that hosts a non-dstack Dante device (Dante Virtual Soundcard, Dante Via) answers the same way and counts as hardware too. Every other source is still named: a PC with no such device, a stale dtray, a dstack device that states `dstack_vers` (also one that announces both kinds).
  - API (additive): `network_floor::arc_records` (every ARC TXT record's name and `dstack_vers`, if any; `arc_versions` keeps its output), `discover::senders::browsers::{Browsers::arc_from, HARDWARE_NS}`.
- **No latency change** for any device or dlink app: P, (c), W and the app buffer (the bridge target) are as in 0.6.145.
- Consumers: dcontroller relays `kind: client` entries of `Discovery::below_floor()` like the peer floor alarm (its own change, told by peer); they name the PC by its device. Its 96 s alarm stops naming Dante hardware once its devices run 0.6.146 (nothing to port). dtray, dasio, intercom, songplayer, camera-box, presenter, obs, iemmixer: move the stack version; nothing to port (none calls `announce_device`).

## 0.6.145

- **Feature (#321, field 15.11.2026, #319): the network floor, a version guard for every dstack binary on the network.** A 3.11 dtray (about v0.6.20, still with the standing mDNS browses that #141 later removed) ran 12 days on two PCs and nothing noticed. Now:
  - `dstack::NETWORK_FLOOR` (`network_floor::Version` 0.6.58, the first build with no standing browse in any binary) is the lowest dstack version allowed on the network. A release that fixes a network-wide defect raises it.
  - Peers: every device's mDNS meter reads the `dstack_vers` TXT of each `_netaudio-arc` answer it hears, and `discover::Discovery` (dcontroller, dtray) checks each device it lists. A dstack device below the floor is warned about once an hour per process: WARN `a dstack device below the network floor: upgrade it` with `kind="peer"`, `name`, `address`, `version`, `floor`. A device without `dstack_vers` is not ours and is never named (so a peer is seen from v0.6.84 on, the first with the key).
  - Local clients: the local status endpoint takes `status <name> <version>` besides a bare `status` (anything else past `status` is refused). dtray and dtray-cli state themselves (`dtray`, `dtray-cli`) and ask again with a bare `status` when an older device refuses it, so a new dtray still reads an old device. A client below the floor gets the same WARN with `kind="client"`, `address=local`.
  - Status: a new `network` object, present once the process's meter runs or a client stated itself: `floor`, `below_floor` (`kind` `peer` with `name`, `address`, `version`; `kind` `client` with `name`, `version`; seen within the hour) and `clients` (`name`, `version`, within the hour). The status may now carry peer names and addresses there.
  - API (additive): module `network_floor` (`Version`, `NETWORK_FLOOR`, `Kind`, `Below`, `Guard`, `Clients`, `Client`, `Warned`, `check`, `warn_below`, `below_of`, `warn_devices`, `arc_versions`, `client_of`, `now_ns`, `HOUR_NS`), `Discovery::below_floor`, `senders::Meter::below_floor`, `ctl::status_request`, `ctl::StatusClient`, `ctl::BAD_CLIENT`, `ctl::probe::{status_as, status_from, client_request}`, `Media::{client_asked, clients, peers_below_floor}`, `dtray::{client, set_client}`.
  - For dcontroller (its own change, asked of it separately): the WARN line and each device's status `network.below_floor` are what it reads to relay an alarm. The peer name in the WARN is logged escaped (`name="..."`).
- **Fix (#319, dasio 15.11.2026): no dstack build can send mDNS refresh queries any more.** An old dtray on two PCs (built before #141) kept standing browses of `_netaudio-*`; its mdns-sd cache re-asked every learned record at 80 % of the 120 s TTL, so every 96 s it sent multicast queries, every Dante host answered in the same second, and a receiver counted late packets in step. The vendored mdns-sd daemon is now answer-only: `browse`, `browse_cache`, `stop_browse`, `resolve_hostname`, `stop_resolve_hostname` and `verify` exist only in its own unit tests, and with no browser the refresh passes have nothing to ask (`vendor/mdns-sd/DSTACK.md`).
